2022  
Annual Report
MESSAGE TO OUR STAKEHOLDERS
More than a decade ago, Aptiv recognized that demands 
for a safer, greener and more connected world would 
reshape the future of the automotive industry, and since 
that time we have continued to reﬁne and execute our 
strategy to capitalize on these megatrends. While the 
recent pandemic and supply chain challenges tested our 
resilience, our ability to meet customer demands and 
achieve record new bookings conﬁrmed the strength of 
our portfolio, the commitment of our global workforce 
and the value of our mission.
Today, the megatrends are accelerating, driven by the 
growing appeal of feature-rich, highly electriﬁed vehicles. 
Our ability to enable these advanced features at a price 
consumers can afford is a testament to our capabilities. 
We are uniquely positioned as the industry’s only full-
system solutions provider, which enables us to optimize 
the performance and cost of the solutions we develop. 
Each day, our products help customers solve their 
toughest challenges, support the sustainability of our 
planet and deliver long-term value for our shareholders. 
While we are proud of the resilient and innovative 
company that we have built, we are even more excited 
about how we are positioned for the future. 
DEMAND ACCELERATING FOR ELECTRIFIED, 
FEATURE-RICH VEHICLES
During 2022, battery electric vehicles (BEVs) accounted 
for approximately 10 percent of new-vehicle production, 
reﬂecting continued strong growth driven by more 
stringent government regulations and increasing 
consumer demand. As a result, we expect the market for 
high-voltage electriﬁcation to increase by more than  
20 percent annually between now and 2030.
The demand for higher levels of vehicle safety also 
increased, which is reﬂected in almost 20 percent of 
new vehicles built including Level 2 or higher advanced 
driver-assistance systems (ADAS). Multiple OEMs have 
now launched hands-free driving applications, many 
of which are enabled by Aptiv solutions that deliver 
greater levels of efﬁciency, ﬂexibility and robustness 
across the broadest possible range of driving scenarios.
2022 also saw a step-function increase in OEMs 
beginning the transition to next-generation hardware 
and software architectures. By 2030, we expect that 
nearly 1 out of every 4 vehicles produced will use a 
highly centralized, software-deﬁned architecture 
approach similar to the one Aptiv has been developing.
A CLEAR PATH FORWARD
Aptiv is leading the transformation in advanced 
vehicle architectures. This effort has been informed 
by the experience of launching more than 200 BEV 
architecture platforms and 60 high-complexity,  
software-intensive programs across more than  
20 OEMs — all while maintaining exceptional levels  
of quality and delivery.
Our Smart Vehicle Architecture™ (SVA™) solution, 
introduced in 2017, comprises hardware and software 
building blocks designed to efﬁciently enable new 
software-deﬁned functionality. We have worked with 
multiple global OEMs on advanced development 
programs. In doing so, we have leveraged our expertise 
in both the “brain” (software and compute) and 
the “nervous system” (power and data distribution) of 
the vehicle to serve as a trusted technology partner. 
This resulted in more than $4 billion in new SVA™ 
business awards during 2022 — up signiﬁcantly from 
less than $1 billion total when we entered the year. We 
continue to be recognized for our innovation. In 2022, 
we won our 25th Automotive News PACE Award for the 
central vehicle controller, a key component of SVA™.
At the same time, Aptiv’s high-voltage solutions are 
reducing weight and mass while also improving range 
and enabling faster charging — critical factors for 
BEV adoption. We have a comprehensive high-voltage 
portfolio, which we expanded during 2022 to include 
integrated power electronics. This solution combines six 
devices into one while also hosting battery management 
system software, and we won our ﬁrst program to 
deliver this integrated solution to a major OEM.
During 2022, Aptiv also acquired an 85 percent 
equity stake in Intercable Automotive Solutions, a 
pioneer in the design and manufacture of high-voltage 
power distribution and interconnect technology. The 
company’s industry-leading busbar technologies 
enhance our high-voltage product portfolio, and 
Intercable Automotive is able to leverage Aptiv’s  
global scale and manufacturing footprint.
THE NEXT CHAPTER FOR APTIV
Consumers increasingly are demanding advanced, 
software-enabled features, which need to be supported 
across the full life cycle of the vehicle. This requires 
a continued evolution of the in-vehicle software 
architecture, as well as the development tools and 
processes OEMs use to enable these solutions. 
We acquired Wind River, a global leader in delivering 
software for mission-critical intelligent systems, whose 
technology is in more than 2 billion edge devices for 
more than 1,700 customers across multiple  
end-markets, including telecommunications, aerospace 
and defense and industrial. Wind River’s proven 
solutions are ideally suited to support next-generation 
vehicle architectures through software abstraction 
and a cloud-native approach that modernizes software 
development, deployment and operation over the life 
cycle of the vehicle.
This acquisition is an enabler for our future, positioning 
us to move away from legacy monolithic architectures 
that have historically deﬁned our industry to a more 
agile, cloud-native approach. This modernizes software 
development and life-cycle management, enables new 
business models and accelerates the industry’s digital 
transformation. As a result, Aptiv is better positioned 
to execute against the attractive opportunities 
in software-deﬁned mobility, and Wind River can 
accelerate its growth in other high-value industries by 
providing its world-class team with additional resources.
POSITIONED FOR THE EMERGING  
INTELLIGENT EDGE
In the future, the vehicle will increasingly become an 
intelligent edge device integrated into the broader 
internet of things ecosystem, enabling more advanced

processing to take place in the cloud, powered by 
machine learning for insights and deep personalization.
We are well positioned to leverage our entire hardware 
and software portfolio to address this expanded 
opportunity. Aptiv’s solutions are not limited to the 
automotive industry, as applications in industrial, 
transportation, renewable energy and other attractive 
markets are being shaped by the same technology 
trends and customer needs, including increasing data, 
power and connectivity requirements. Looking ahead, 
our ability to serve these markets nearly doubles our 
total addressable market opportunity to $400 billion  
by 2030.
REALIZING OUR VISION FOR TODAY AND BEYOND
The strategy we developed roughly a decade ago has 
proved to be the right one, helping us stay focused 
on our goals and allowing us to successfully navigate 
the challenges facing our industry. Our priority remains 
to deliver on our commitments to our customers and 
our shareholders — both today and in the future. 
I offer my deepest thanks to our 200,000 employees 
around the world, whose determination and sacriﬁces 
have positioned us for the future, and I thank all our 
stakeholders for their continued support of our mission.
Kevin P. Clark
Chairman and Chief Executive Ofﬁcer

Supply Relationships with Our Customers
We typically supply products to our OEM customers through purchase orders, which are generally governed by general
terms and conditions establ
a ished by each OEM. Although the terms and conditions vary
r frf om customer to customer, they
typically contemplate a relationship under which our customers place orders for
f
their requirements of specifif c components
supplied for
f
particular vehicles but are not required to purchase any minimum amount of products frf om us. These relationships
typically extend over the lifef of the related vehicle. Prices are negotiated with respect to each business award, which may be
subject to adjustments under certain circumstances, such as commodity or for
f
eign exchange escalation/de-escalation clauses or
for
f
cost reductions achieved by us. The terms and conditions typically provide that we are subject to a warranty on the products
supplied; in most cases, the duration of such warranty is coterminous with the warranty offf ef red by the OEM to the end-user of
the vehicle. We may also be obligated to share in all or a part of recall costs if the OEM recalls its vehicles for
f
defef cts
attributabl
a e to our products.
Individual purchase orders are terminabl
a e for
f
cause or non-perfor
f
mance and, in most cases, upon our insolvency and
certain change of control events. In addition, many of our OEM customers have the option to terminate for
f
convenience on
certain programs, which permits our customers to impose pressure on pricing during the lifef of the vehicle program, and issue
purchase contracts for
f
less than the duration of the vehicle program, which potentially reduces our profif t margins and increases
the risk of our losing fut
f ur
t
e sales under those purchase contracts. We manufaf ctur
t
e and ship based on customer release
schedules, normally provided on a weekly basis, which can vary
r due to cyclical automobile production or dealer inventory
r
levels.
Although customer programs typically extend to fut
f ur
t
e periods, and although there is an expectation that we will supply
certain levels of OEM production during such fut
f ur
t
e periods, customer agreements including appl
a
icabl
a e terms and conditions
do not necessarily constitut
t e fif rm orders. Firm orders are generally limited to specifif c and authorized customer purchase order
releases placed with our manufaf ctur
t
ing and distribution centers for
f
actua
t
l production and order ful
f fif llment. Firm orders are
typically ful
f fif lled as promptly as possible frf om the conversion of availabl
a e raw materials, sub-components and work-in-process
inventory
r for
f
OEM orders and frf om current on-hand fif nished goods inventory
r for
f
aftf ermarket orders. The dollar amount of such
purchase order releases on hand and not processed at any point in time is not believed to be signifif cant based upon the time
frf ame involved.
Materials
We procure our raw materials frf om a variety of suppliers around the world. Generally, we seek to obtain materials in the
region in which our products are manufaf ctur
t
ed in order to minimize transportation and other costs. The most signifif cant raw
materials we use to manufaf ctur
t
e our products include copper and resins. As of December 31, 2022, we have not experienced
any signifif cant shortages of raw materials, however, as a result of our customers’ recent production volatility and cancellations,
our balance of productive, raw and component material inventories has increased substantially frf om customary
r levels. These
changes to the production environment have been primarily driven by the worldwide semiconductor shortage. We continue to
actively monitor and manage inventory
r levels across all inventory
r types in order to maximize both supply continuity and the
10
efff if cient use of working capi
a tal. Normally we do not carry
r inventories of such raw materials in excess of those reasonabl
a y
required to meet our production and shipping schedules.
Commodity cost volatility, most notabl
a y related to copper, petroleum-based resin products and fue
f
l, is a challenge for
f
us
and our industry.
r
Recently, the industry
r has been subjected to increased pricing pressures, specifif cally in relation to these
commodities, which have experienced signifif cant volatility in price. We have also been impacted globally by increased overall
inflf ation as a result of a variety of global trends. We are continually seeking to manage these and other material-related cost
pressures using a combination of strategies, including working with our suppliers to mitigate costs, seeking alternative product
designs and material specifif cations, combining our purchase requirements with our customers and/or suppliers, changing
suppliers, hedging of certain commodities and other means. In the case of copper, which primarily afff ef cts our Signal and Power
Solutions segment, contract clauses have enabl
a ed us to pass on some of the price increases to our customers and thereby
partially offf sf et the impact of increased commodity costs on operating income for
f
the related products. Other than in the case of
copper, our overall success in passing commodity cost increases on to our customers has been limited. However, in 2022, we
have negotiated, and will continue to negotiate, price increases with our customers in response to the global supply chain
disrupt
r
ions impacting the automotive industry.
r
We will continue our efff or
f
ts to pass market-driven commodity cost increases to
our customers in an efff or
f
t to mitigate all or some of the adverse earnings impacts, including by seeking to renegotiate terms as
contracts with our customers expire.
Seasonality
In general, our business is moderately seasonal, as our primary
r North American customers historically reduce production
during the month of July and halt operations for
f
appr
a
oximately one week in December. Our European customers generally
reduce production during the months of July and August and for
f
one week in December. Our Chinese customers generally halt
operations for
f
one week during the months of Februa
r
ry
r and October. Shut-down periods in the rest of the world generally vary
r
by country.
r
In addition, automotive production is traditionally reduced in the months of July, August and September due to the
launch of component production for
f
new vehicle models.

our offf if ces. We are creating packaging that uses less material and we continue to strive to increase the share of waste and excess

Maintaining a strong position in the Chinese market is a key component of our global growth strategy. The automotive
technology and components market in China is highly competitive, with competition frf om many of the largest global
manufaf ctur
t
ers and numerous smaller domestic manufaf ctur
t
ers. As the size of the Chinese market continues to increase over the
long-term, we anticipate that additional competitors, both international and domestic, will seek to enter the Chinese market and
that existing market participants will act aggressively to increase their market share. Increased competition may result in price
reductions, reduced margins and our inabi
a lity to gain or hold market share. Additionally, there have been periods of increased
market volatility and moderations in the level of economic growth in China, which resulted in periods of lower automotive
production growth rates in China than those previously experienced. Our business in China is sensitive to economic and market
conditions that drive automotive sales volumes in China and may be impacted if there are reductions in vehicle demand in
China. For example, in 2022, various regions in China, including regions where Aptiv has operations, were subjected to
lockdowns imposed by governmental authorities to mitigate the spread of COVID-19, which resulted in industry-
r

production interrupt

these lockdowns during 2022 was appr
a

or if vehicle sales in China continue to experience minimal growth or decrease, our business and fif nancial results could be
materially adversely afff ef cted.

r agencies. Although there are difff ef ring rul
r es and
regulations across countries governing recalls for
f
safef ty issues, the overall transition towards global vehicle platfor
f
ms may also
contribute to increased recalls outside of the U.S., as automotive components are increasingly standardized across regions.
Given the sensitivity to safef ty issues in the automotive industry,
r
including increased foc
f
us frf om regulators and consumers, we
anticipate the number of automotive recalls may remain above
a
historical levels in the near fut
f ur
t
e. Although we engage in
extensive product quality programs and processes, it is possible that we may be adversely afff ef cted in the fut
f ur
t
e if the pace of
these recalls continues.
Ef
E ff if cient use of capital. The global vehicle components industry
r is generally capi
a tal intensive and a portion of a supplier’s
capi
a tal equipment is frf equently utilized for
f
specifif c customer programs. Lead times for
f
procurement of capi
a tal equipment are
long and typically exceed start of production by one to two years. Substantial advantages exist for
f
suppliers that can leverage
their prior investments in capi
a tal equipment or amortize the investment over higher volume global customer programs.
Indus
I
trt y
r consolidation and disi ruptive new entrt antst . Consolidation among worldwide OEMs and suppliers is expected to
continue as these companies seek to achieve operating synergies and value stream efff if ciencies, acquire complementary
r
technologies and build stronger customer relationships. Additionally, the rise of advanced softf ware and technologies in vehicles
has attracted new and disrupt
r
ive entrants frf om outside the traditional automotive supply industry.
r
These entrants may seek to
gain access to certain vehicle technology and component markets. Any of these new competitors may develop and introduce
technologies that gain greater customer or consumer acceptance, which could adversely afff ef ct the fut
f ur
t
e growth of the
Company. We believe companies with strong balance sheets and fif nancial discipline are in the best position to take advantage
of these trends.

a e or unfaf vorabl
a e
impacts on revenue. Such changes can be the result of shiftf s in regional growth, shiftf s in OEM sales demand, as well as shiftf s in
consumer demand related to vehicle segment purchases and content penetration. For instance, a shiftf in sales demand faf voring a
particular OEM’s vehicle model for

The automotive technology and component supply industry
r is traditionally subject to inflf ationary
r pressures with respect
to raw materials and labor
a
which may place operational and profif tabi
a lity burdens on the entire supply chain. For instance, the
industry
r has recently been subjected to increased pricing pressures, specifif cally in relation to copper and petroleum-based resin
products, which have experienced signifif cant volatility in price. We have also been impacted globally by increased overall
39
inflf ation as a result of a variety of global trends. Due to various faf ctors, the industry
r is also faf cing increased operating and
logistics challenges frf om certain global supply chain disrupt
r
ions, including a worldwide semiconductor supply shortage. This
shortage has resulted in increased pricing pressures on semiconductors as well. In addition, we expect semiconductor supply
cost and commodity cost volatility to have a continual impact on fut
f ur
t
e earnings and/or operating cash flf ows. As such, we
continually seek to mitigate both inflf ationary
r pressures and our material-related cost exposures using a number of appr
a
oaches,
including combining purchase requirements with customers and/or other suppliers, using alternate suppliers or product designs,
negotiating cost reductions and/or commodity cost contract escalation clauses into our vehicle manufaf ctur
t
er supply contracts
and hedging. We have also negotiated, and will continue to negotiate, price increases with our customers in response to the
afor
f
ementioned global supply chain disrupt
r
ions.

Results of Operations by Segment
We operate our core business along the fol
f lowing operating segments, which are grouped on the basis of similar product,
market and operating faf ctors:
•
Signal and Power Solutions, which includes complete electrical architectur
t
e and component products.
•
Advanced Safef ty and User Experience, which includes vehicle technology and services in advanced safef ty, user
experience and connectivity and security solutions, as well as cloud-native softf ware platfor
f
ms, autonomous driving
technologies and DevOps tools.
•
Eliminations and Other, which includes i) the elimination of inter-segment transactions, and ii) certain other expenses
and income of a non-operating or strategic natur
t
e.
Generally, Aptiv evaluates segment perfor
f
mance based on stand-alone segment net income befor
f
e interest expense, other
income (expense), net, income tax (expense) benefif t, equity income (loss), net of tax, amortization, restruc
r
tur
t
ing, other
acquisition and portfol
f io project costs (which includes costs incurred to integrate acquired businesses and to plan and execute
product portfol
f io transfor
f
mation actions, including business and product acquisitions and divestitur
t
es), asset impairments and
other related charges and gains (losses) on business divestitur
t
es and other transactions (“Adjusted Operating Income”) and
accounts for
f
inter-segment sales and transfef rs as if the sales or transfef rs were to third parties, at current market prices.
Efff ef ctive on January
r 1, 2022, the Company now excludes amortization expense of intangible assets frf om the calculation
of Adjusted Operating Income, as reflf ected in the defif nition below. The Company’s management believes that the updated
calculation of this fif nancial measure will be more useful
f
to both management and investors in their analysis of the Company’s
results of operations due to recent acquisitions. Amortization of intangible assets generally results frf om a write-up in the value
of assets in connection with an acquisition. The Company believes that exclusion of amortization expense will faf cilitate more
comparabl
a e operating results of the Company over time, between periods when the Company is more or less acquisitive and
allows for
f
improved comparison with both acquisitive and non-acquisitive peer companies. The historical presentation of
Adjusted Operating Income in the tabl
a es below has been revised to be consistent with this updated calculation.
Our management utilizes segment Adjusted Operating Income as the key perfor
f
mance measure of segment income or loss
to evaluate segment perfor
f
mance, and for
f
planning and for
f
ecasting purpos
r
es to allocate resources to the segments, as
management believes this measure is most reflf ective of the operational profif tabi
a lity or loss of our operating segments. Segment
44
Adjusted Operating Income should not be considered a substitut
t e for
f
results prepared in accordance with U.S. GAAP and
should not be considered an alternative to net income attributabl
a e to Aptiv, which is the most directly comparabl
a e fif nancial
measure to Adjusted Operating Income that is prepared in accordance with U.S. GAAP. Segment Adjusted Operating Income,
as determined and measured by Aptiv, should also not be compared to similarly titled measures reported by other companies.

Cr
C editi Agreement
Aptiv PLC and its wholly-owned subsidiary,
r
Aptiv Corpor
r
ation, entered into a credit agreement (the “Credit Agreement”)
with JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A., as administrative agent (the “Administrative Agent”), under which it maintains senior
unsecured credit faf cilities currently consisting of a term loan (the “Tranche A Term Loan”) and a revolving credit faf cility of $2
billion (the “Revolving Credit Facility”). Subsequently, Aptiv Global Financing Limited (“AGFL”), a wholly-owned subsidiary
r
of Aptiv PLC, executed a joinder agreement to the Credit Agreement, which allows it to act as a borrower under the Credit
Agreement, and a guaranty supplement, under which AGFL guarantees the obligations under the Credit Agreement, subject to
certain exceptions.
The Credit Agreement was entered into in March 2011 and has been subsequently amended and restated on several
occasions, most recently on June 24, 2021. The June 2021 amendment, among other things, (1) refif nanced and replaced the
existing term loan A and revolver with a new term loan A that matur
t
es in 2026, and a new fif ve-year revolving credit faf cility
with aggregate commitments of $2 billion, (2) utilized the Company’s existing sustainabi
a lity-linked metrics and commitments,
that, if achieved, would change the faf cility fef e and interest rate margins as described below, and (3) establ
a ished the leverage
ratio maintenance covenant that requires the Company to maintain total net leverage (as calculated in accordance with the
Credit Agreement) of less than 3.5 to 1.0 (or 4.0 to 1.0 for
f
four
f
ful
f l fif scal quarters fol
f lowing completion of material
acquisitions, as defif ned in the Credit Agreement) and allowed for
f
dividends and other payments on equity. Losses on
modifif cation of debt totaled $1 million and $4 million during the years ended December 31, 2021 and 2020, respectively,
related to the June 2021 amendment and May 2020 amendment. Aptiv paid amendment fef es of $6 million and $18 million

Signifif cant Accounting Policies and Critical Accounting Estimates
Our signifif cant accounting policies are described in Note 2. Signifif cant Accounting Policies to the audited consolidated
fif nancial statements included herein. Certain of our accounting policies require the appl
a
ication of signifif cant judgment by
management in selecting the appr
a
opriate assumptions for
f
calculating fif nancial estimates. By their natur
t
e, these judgments are
subject to an inherent degree of uncertainty, which is fur
f
ther exacerba
r
ted by, among other faf ctors, the unknown fut
f ur
t
e duration
and severity of the impacts of the ongoing global supply chain disrupt
r
ions and the conflf ict between Ukraine and Rus
R
sia. These
judgments are based on our historical experience, terms of existing contracts, our evaluation of trends in the industry,
r
infor
f
mation provided by our customers and infor
f
mation availabl
a e frf om other outside sources, as appr
a
opriate.
We consider an accounting estimate to be critical if:f
•
It requires us to make assumptions about
a
matters that were uncertain at the time we were making the estimate, and
•
Changes in the estimate or difff ef rent estimates that we could have selected would have had a material impact on our
fif nancial condition or results of operations.
Acquisi iti it ons and Oth
t er Tr
T ansactit ons
In accordance with the accounting guidance in Financial Accounting Standards Board (“FASB”) Accounting Standards
Codifif cation (“ASC”) Topic 805, Business Com
C
binations, we allocate the purchase price of an acquired business to its
identifif abl
a e assets and liabi
a lities based on estimated faf ir values. The excess of the purchase price over the amount allocated to
the assets and liabi
a lities, if any, is recorded as goodwill. The process to estimate faf ir value described herein is generally
appl
a
icabl
a e to other transactions, including the faf ir value estimates used in establ
a ishing the identifif abl
a e assets, liabi
a lities and
goodwill recorded upon for
f
mation of Motional, Aptiv’s autonomous driving joint ventur
t
e, and the resulting equity method
investment recorded on Aptiv’s balance sheet.
An acquisition may include a contingent consideration component. The faf ir value of the contingent consideration is
estimated as of the date of the acquisition and is recorded as part of the purchase price. This estimate is updated in fut
f ur
t
e
periods and any changes in the estimate, which are not considered an adjustment to the purchase price, are recorded in our
consolidated statements of operations.
An acquisition may also include a redeemabl
a e noncontrolling interest component. The faf ir value of the noncontrolling
interest is recorded to temporary
r equity in the consolidated balance sheet and is estimated as of the date of acquisition using a
Monte Carlo simulation appr
a
oach, which includes several assumptions including estimated fut
f ur
t
e profif tabi
a lity, expected
volatility rate and risk frf ee rate. The redeemabl
a e noncontrolling interest is then adjusted each reporting period for
f
the income
(loss) attributabl
a e to the noncontrolling interest, and for
f
any measurement period adjustments necessary
r to record the
redeemabl
a e noncontrolling interest at the higher of its redemption value, assuming it was redeemabl
a e at the reporting date, or its
carryi
r ng value. Any measurement period adjustments are recorded to retained earnings, with a corresponding increase or
reduction to net income attributabl
a e to Aptiv.
We use all availabl
a e infor
f
mation to estimate faf ir values. We typically engage outside appr
a
aisal fif rms to assist in the faf ir
value determination of identifif abl
a e intangible assets and any other signifif cant assets or liabi
a lities. We adjust the preliminary
r
purchase price allocation, as necessary,
r
up to one year aftf er the acquisition closing date as we obtain more infor
f
mation
regarding asset valuations and liabi
a lities assumed.
Our purchase price allocation methodology contains uncertainties because it requires management to make assumptions
and to appl
a
y judgment to estimate the faf ir value of acquired assets and liabi
a lities. Management estimates the faf ir value of assets
and liabi
a lities based upon quoted market prices, the carryi
r ng value of the acquired assets and widely accepted valuation
techniques, including discounted cash flf ows and market multiple analyses. Unanticipated events or circumstances may occur
which could afff ef ct the accuracy of our faf ir value estimates, including assumptions regarding industry
r economic faf ctors and
business strategies.
Other estimates used in determining faf ir value include, but are not limited to, fut
f ur
t
e cash flf ows or income related to
intangibles, market rate assumptions, actua
t
rial assumptions for
f
benefif t plans and appr
a
opriate discount rates. Our estimates of
faf ir value are based upon assumptions believed to be reasonabl
a e, but that are inherently uncertain, and therefor
f
e, may not be
realized. Accordingly, there can be no assurance that the estimates, assumptions, and values reflf ected in the valuations will be
realized, and actua
t
l results could vary
r materially.

Estimating warranty obligations requires us to for
f
ecast the resolution of existing claims and expected fut
f ur
t
e claims on
products sold. We base our estimate on historical trends of units sold and payment amounts, combined with our current
understanding of the status
t
of existing claims and discussions with our customers. The key faf ctors which impact our estimates
are (1) the stated or implied warranty period; (2) OEM source; (3) OEM policy decisions regarding warranty claims; and
(4) OEMs seeking to hold suppliers responsible for
f
product warranties. These estimates are re-evaluated on an ongoing basis.

ITEM 7A. QUANTITATIVE AND QUALITATIVE DISCLOSURES ABOUT MARKET RISK
We are exposed to market risks frf om changes in currency exchange rates and certain commodity prices. In order to
manage these risks, we operate a centralized risk management program that consists of entering into a variety of derivative
contracts with the intent of mitigating our risk to flf uctua
t
tions in currency exchange rates and commodity prices. We do not
enter into derivative transactions for
f
speculative or trading purpos
r
es.
A discussion of our accounting policies for
f
derivative instrum
r
ents is included in Note 2. Signifif cant Accounting Policies
to the audited consolidated fif nancial statements included herein and fur
f
ther disclosure is provided in Note 17. Derivatives and
Hedging Activities to the audited consolidated fif nancial statements included herein. We maintain risk management control
systems to monitor exchange and commodity risks and related hedge positions. Positions are monitored using a variety of
analytical techniques including market value and sensitivity analysis. The fol
f lowing analyses are based on sensitivity tests,
which assume instantaneous, parallel shiftf s in currency exchange rates and commodity prices. For options and instrum
r
ents with
non-linear retur
t
ns, appr
a
opriate models are utilized to determine the impact of shiftf s in rates and prices.
We have currency exposures related to buying, selling and fif nancing in currencies other than the local currencies in which
we operate. Historically, we have reduced our exposure through fif nancial instrum
r
ents (hedges) that provide offf sf ets or limits to
our exposures, which are opposite to the underlying transactions. We also faf ce an inherent business risk of exposure to
commodity prices risks, and have historically offf sf et our exposure, particularly to changes in the price of various non-fef rrous
metals used in our manufaf ctur
t
ing operations, through fif xed price purchase agreements, commodity swaps
a
and option contracts.
We continue to manage our exposures to changes in currency rates and commodity prices using these derivative instrum
r
ents.

We have audited the accompanying consolidated balance sheets of Aptiv PLC (the Company) as of December 31, 2022 and

Basis for
f
Opinion
These fif nancial statements are the responsibility of the Company’s management. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on
the Company’s fif nancial statements based on our audits. We are a public accounting fif rm registered with the PCAOB and are
required to be independent with respect to the Company in accordance with the U.S. fef deral securities laws and the appl

whether the fif nancial statements are frf ee of material misstatement, whether due to

statements, whether due to error or frf aud, and perfor
f
ming procedures that respond to those risks. Such procedures included
examining, on a test basis, evidence regarding the amounts and disclosures in the fif nancial statements. Our audits also included
evaluating the accounting principles used and signifif cant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall
presentation of the fif nancial statements. We believe that our audits provide a reasonabl
a e basis for
f
our opinion.
Critical Audit Matters
The critical audit matters communicated below are matters arising frf om the current period audit of the consolidated fif nancial
statements that were communicated or required to be communicated to the audit committee and that: (1) relate to accounts or
disclosures that are material to the fif nancial statements and (2) involved our especially challenging, subjective, or complex
judgments. The communication of critical audit matters does not alter in any way our opinion on the consolidated fif nancial
statements, taken as a whole, and we are not, by communicating the critical audit matters below, providing separate opinions on
the critical audit matters or on the accounts or disclosures to which they relate.
Uncertain Tax Positions
Descript
i ion
of the
t
Mat
M ter
As described in Notes 2 and 14, the Company establ
a ishes reserves for
f
uncertain tax positions for
f
positions that
are taken on their income tax retur
t
ns that might not be sustained upon examination by the taxing authorities. At
December 31, 2022, the Company has recorded appr
a
oximately $224 million relating to uncertain tax positions.
In determining whether an uncertain tax position exists, the Company determines, based solely on its technical
merits, whether the tax position is more likely than not to be sustained upon examination, and if so, a tax benefif t
is measured on a cumulative probabi
a lity basis that is more likely than not to be realized upon the ultimate
settlement. The Company identifif es its certain and uncertain tax positions and then evaluates the recognition and
measurement steps to determine the amount that should be recognized. The Company then evaluates uncertain
tax positions in subsequent periods for
f
recognition, de-recognition or re-measurement if changes have occurred,
or when efff ef ctive settlement or expiration of the statut
t e of limitations occurs.
62
Auditing the uncertain tax positions is complex because of the judgmental natur
t
e of the tax accrua
r
ls and various
other tax retur
t
n positions that might not be sustained upon review by taxing authorities. The Company fif les tax
retur
t
ns in multiple jurisdictions and is subject to examination by taxing authorities throughout the world due to
its complex global foot
f
pr
t
int. Taxing jurisdictions signifif cant to Aptiv include Barba
r
dos, China, Germany,
Ireland, Luxembourg, Mexico, South Korea, the U.K. and the U.S.
How
H
We
W
Addressed
the
t
Mat
M ter
in Our Audit
We obtained an understanding, evaluated the design, and tested the operating efff ef ctiveness of controls related to
the recognition, measurement and the evaluation of changes in uncertain tax positions. This included testing
controls over management’s review of the tax positions, their evaluation of whether they met the measurement
threshold and then recalculating the amounts recognized based upon a cumulative probabi
a lity assessment
perfor
f
med by management.
Our audit procedures to test the Company’s uncertain tax positions included, among others, involvement of our
tax profef ssionals, including transfef r pricing profef ssionals. This included evaluating tax opinions and third-party
transfef r pricing studi
t
es obtained by the Company and assessing the Company’s correspondence with the
relevant tax authorities. We analyzed the Company’s assumptions and data used to determine the amount of tax
benefif t to recognize and tested the accuracy of the calculations. Our testing also included the evaluation of the
ongoing positions and consideration of changes, the recording of penalties and interest and the ultimate
settlement and payment of certain tax matters.
Revenue Recognition
Descript
i ion
of the
t
Mat
M ter
As described in Notes 2 and 24, Aptiv occasionally enters into pricing agreements with its customers that
provide for
f
price reductions, some of which are conditional upon achieving certain joint cost saving targets. In
addition, frf om time to time, Aptiv makes payments to customers in conjunction with ongoing business. Revenue
is recognized based on the agreed-upon price at the time of shipment, and sales incentives, allowances and
certain customer payments are recognized as a reduction to revenue at the time of the commitment to provide
such incentives or make these payments. Certain other customer payments or upfrf ont fef es are considered to be a
cost to obtain a contract as they are directly attributabl
a e to a contract, are incremental and management expects
the payments to be recoverabl
a e. In these cases, the customer payment is capi
a talized and amortized to revenue
based on the transfef r of goods and services to the customer for
f
which the upfrf ont payment relates. As of
December 31, 2022, Aptiv has recorded $78 million related to these capi
a talized upfrf ont payments.
Auditing the accounting for
f
and completeness of arrangements containing elements such as sales incentives,
allowances and customer payments, including the appr
a
opriate timing and presentation of adjustments to revenue
as well as costs to obtain a contract is judgmental due to the unique faf cts and circumstances involved in each
revenue arrangement, as well as on-going commercial negotiations with customers.
How
H
We
W
Addressed
the
t
Mat
M ter
in Our Audit
We obtained an understanding, evaluated the design and tested the operating efff ef ctiveness of controls over the
review of customer contracts. This included testing controls over management’s process to identify
f and evaluate
customer contracts that contain sales incentives, allowances and customer payments that impact revenue
recognition.
Our audit procedures to test the completeness of the Company’s identifif cation of such contracts included,
among others, interviewing sales representatives who are responsible for
f
negotiations with customers and
testing cash payments and credit memos issued to customers. To test management’s assessment of customer
contracts containing sales incentives, allowances and customer payments, our procedures included, among
others, selecting a sample of customer agreements, obtaining and reviewing source documentation, including
master agreements, and other documents that were part of the agreement, and evaluating the contract terms to
determine the appr
a
opriateness of the accounting treatment.

Descript

of the

M ter
As described in Note 20, Aptiv completed the acquisition of Wind River Systems, Inc. (“Wind River”) on

based on third-party valuations and management’s estimates, generally utilizing income and market appr
a
oaches.
Auditing the Company's valuation of technology-related and customer-based intangible assets was complex and
required signifif cant auditor judgment due to the high degree of subjectivity in evaluating certain assumptions
required to estimate the faf ir value of these intangible assets. The faf ir value measurement was sensitive to

the
t

M ter
in Our Audit

Our audit procedures to test the estimated faf ir value of the acquired technology-related and customer-based

Report of Independent Registered Public Accounting Firm
To the Shareholders and the Board of Directors of Aptiv PLC
Opinion on Internal Control Over Financial Reporting
We have audited Aptiv PLC’s internal control over fif nancial reporting as of December 31, 2022, based on criteria establ
a ished in
Internal Control-Integrated Framework issued by the Committee of Sponsoring Organizations of the Treadway Commission
(2013 frf amework) (the COSO criteria). In our opinion, Aptiv PLC (the Company) maintained, in all material respects, efff ef ctive
internal control over fif nancial reporting as of December 31, 2022, based on the COSO criteria.
As indicated in the accompanying Management’s Report on Internal Control Over Financial Reporting, management's
assessment of and conclusion on the efff ef ctiveness of internal control over fif nancial reporting did not include the internal
controls of Wind River Systems, Inc. or Intercabl
a e Automotive Solutions S.r.l, which are included in the 2022 consolidated
fif nancial statements of the Company and constitut
t ed 23% of total assets as of December 31, 2022 and less than 1% of net sales
and net income for
f
the year then ended. Our audit of internal control over fif nancial reporting of the Company also did not
include an evaluation of the internal control over fif nancial reporting of Wind River Systems, Inc. or Intercabl
a e Automotive
Solutions S.r.l.
We also have audited, in accordance with the standards of the Public Company Accounting Oversight Board (United States)
(PCAOB), the consolidated balance sheets of the Company as of December 31, 2022 and 2021, and the related consolidated
statements of operations, comprehensive income, shareholders’ equity and cash flf ows for
f
each of the three years in the period
ended December 31, 2022, and the related notes and fif nancial statement schedule and our report dated Februa
r
ry
r 8, 2023
expressed an unqualifif ed opinion thereon.
Basis for
f
Opinion
The Company’s management is responsible for
f
maintaining efff ef ctive internal control over fif nancial reporting and for
f
its
assessment of the efff ef ctiveness of internal control over fif nancial reporting included in the accompanying Management’s Report
on Internal Control over Financial Reporting. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on the Company’s internal control
over fif nancial reporting based on our audit. We are a public accounting fif rm registered with the PCAOB and are required to be
independent with respect to the Company in accordance with the U.S. fef deral securities laws and the appl
a
icabl
a e rul
r es and
regulations of the Securities and Exchange Commission and the PCAOB.
We conducted our audit in accordance with the standards of the PCAOB. Those standards require that we plan and perfor
f
m the
audit to obtain reasonabl
a e assurance about
a
whether efff ef ctive internal control over fif nancial reporting was maintained in all
material respects.
Our audit included obtaining an understanding of internal control over fif nancial reporting, assessing the risk that a material
weakness exists, testing and evaluating the design and operating efff ef ctiveness of internal control based on the assessed risk, and
perfor
f
ming such other procedures as we considered necessary
r in the circumstances. We believe that our audit provides a
reasonabl
a e basis for
f
our opinion.
Defif nition and Limitations of Internal Control Over Financial Reporting
A company’s internal control over fif nancial reporting is a process designed to provide reasonabl
a e assurance regarding the
reliabi
a lity of fif nancial reporting and the preparation of fif nancial statements for
f
external purpos
r
es in accordance with generally
accepted accounting principles. A company’s internal control over fif nancial reporting includes those policies and procedures
that (1) pertain to the maintenance of records that, in reasonabl
a e detail, accurately and faf irly reflf ect the transactions and
dispositions of the assets of the company; (2) provide reasonabl
a e assurance that transactions are recorded as necessary
r to permit
preparation of fif nancial statements in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles, and that receipts and
expenditur
t
es of the company are being made only in accordance with authorizations of management and directors of the
company; and (3) provide reasonabl
a e assurance regarding prevention or timely detection of unauthorized acquisition, use, or
disposition of the company’s assets that could have a material efff ef ct on the fif nancial statements.
65
Because of its inherent limitations, internal control over fif nancial reporting may not prevent or detect misstatements. Also,
projections of any evaluation of efff ef ctiveness to fut
f ur
t
e periods are subject to the risk that controls may become inadequate
because of changes in conditions, or that the degree of compliance with the policies or procedures may deteriorate.

1. GENERA
R L
General and basis of presentation—“Aptiv,” the “Company,” “we,” “us” and “our” refef r to Aptiv PLC (for
f
merly
known as Delphi Automotive PLC), a public limited company for
f
med under the laws of Jersey on May 19, 2011, which
completed an initial public offf ef ring on November 22, 2011, and its consolidated subsidiaries. The Company’s ordinary
r shares
are publicly traded on the New York Stock Exchange under the symbol “APTV.”
The consolidated fif nancial statements have been prepared in accordance with accounting principles generally accepted in
the United States of America (“U.S. GAAP”).
Nature of operations—Aptiv is a leading global technology and mobility architectur
t
e company primarily serving the
automotive sector. We deliver end-to-end mobility solutions enabl
a ing our customers' transition to more electrifif ed, softf ware-
defif ned vehicles. We design and manufaf ctur
t
e vehicle components and provide electrical, electronic and active safef ty
technology solutions to the global automotive and commercial vehicle markets. Aptiv is one of the largest vehicle technology
suppliers and our customers include the 25 largest automotive original equipment manufaf ctur
t
ers (“OEMs”) in the world. Aptiv
operates 131 maja or manufaf ctur
t
ing faf cilities and 11 maja or technical centers utilizing a regional service model that enabl
a es the
Company to efff if ciently and efff ef ctively serve its global customers frf om best cost countries. Aptiv has a presence in 48 countries
and has appr
a
oximately 22,000 scientists, engineers and technicians foc
f
used on developing market relevant product solutions for
f
its customers.
2. SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES
Consolidation—The consolidated fif nancial statements include the accounts of Aptiv and the subsidiaries in which Aptiv
holds a controlling fif nancial or management interest and variabl
a e interest entities of which Aptiv has determined that it is the
primary
r benefif ciary.
r
Aptiv’s share of the earnings or losses of non-controlled afff if liates, over which Aptiv exercises signifif cant
inflf uence (generally a 20% to 50% ownership interest), is included in the consolidated operating results using the equity method
of accounting. When Aptiv does not have the abi
a lity to exercise signifif cant inflf uence (generally when ownership interest is less
than 20%), investments in non-consolidated afff if liates without readily determinabl
a e faf ir value are measured at cost, less
impairments, adjusted for
f
observabl
a e price changes in orderly transactions for
f
identical or similar investments of the same
issuer, while investments in publicly traded equity securities are measured at faf ir value based on quoted prices for
f
identical
assets on active market exchanges as of each reporting date. The Company monitors its investments in afff if liates for
f
indicators
of other-than-temporary
r declines in value on an ongoing basis. If the Company determines that such a decline has occurred, an
impairment loss is recorded, which is measured as the difff ef rence between carryi
r ng value and estimated faf ir value. Estimated
faf ir value is generally determined using an income appr
a
oach based on discounted cash flf ows or negotiated transaction values.
Intercompany transactions and balances between consolidated Aptiv businesses have been eliminated.
During the years ended December 31, 2022, 2021 and 2020, Aptiv received dividends of $5 million, $6 million and $9
million, respectively, frf om one of its equity method investments. The dividends were recognized as a reduction to the
investment and represented a retur
t
n on investment in cash flf ows frf om operating activities.
Aptiv's equity investments without readily determinabl
a e faf ir value totaled $67 million and $30 million as of
December 31, 2022 and 2021, respectively, and are classifif ed within other long-term assets in the consolidated balance sheets.
Aptiv's investments in publicly traded equity securities totaled $17 million and $66 million as of December 31, 2022 and 2021,
respectively, and are classifif ed within other long-term assets in the consolidated balance sheet. Refef r to Note 5. Investments in
Afff if liates for
f
fur
f
ther infor
f
mation regarding Aptiv's equity investments.

Use of estimates—Preparation of consolidated fif nancial statements in confor
f
mity with U.S. GAAP requires the use of
estimates and assumptions that afff ef ct amounts reported therein. Generally, matters subject to estimation and judgment include
amounts related to accounts receivabl
a e realization, inventory
r obsolescence, asset impairments, useful
f
lives of intangible and
fif xed assets, defef rred tax asset valuation allowances, income taxes, pension benefif t plan assumptions, accrua
r
ls related to
litigation, warranty costs, environmental remediation costs, contingent consideration arrangements, redeemabl
a e noncontrolling
interest, worker’s compensation accrua
r
ls and healthcare accrua
r

including the duration and severity of the impacts of the ongoing global supply chain disrupt
r
ions and the conflf ict between
Ukraine and Rus
R
sia, actua
t
l results reported in fut
f ur
t

Revenue recognition—Revenue is measured based on consideration specifif ed in a contract with a customer. Customer
contracts for
f
production parts generally are represented by a combination of a current purchase order and a current production
schedule issued by the customer. Customer contracts for
f
softf ware licenses are generally represented by a sales contract or
purchase order. The Company recognizes revenue when it satisfif es a perfor
f
mance obligation by transfef rring control over a
product or service to a customer. Revenue frf om softf ware licenses is generally recognized at a point in time upon delivery
r while
revenue frf om post delivery
r support and maintenance for
f
softf ware contracts are recognized over time on a ratabl
a e basis over the
contract term. From time to time, Aptiv enters into pricing agreements with its customers that provide for
f
price reductions,
some of which are conditional upon achieving certain joint cost saving targets. In these instances, revenue is recognized based
on the agreed-upon price at the time of shipment.
Sales incentives and allowances are recognized as a reduction to revenue at the time of the related sale. In addition, frf om
time to time, Aptiv makes payments to customers in conjunction with ongoing business. These payments to customers are
generally recognized as a reduction to revenue at the time of the commitment to make these payments. However, certain other
payments to customers, or upfrf ont fef es, meet the criteria to be considered a cost to obtain a contract as they are directly
attributabl
a e to a contract, are incremental and management expects the fef es to be recoverabl
a e.
Aptiv collects and remits taxes assessed by difff ef rent governmental authorities that are both imposed on and concurrent
with a revenue-producing transaction between the Company and the Company’s customers. These taxes may include, but are
not limited to, sales, use, value-added, and some excise taxes. Aptiv reports the collection of these taxes on a net basis
(excluded frf om revenues). Shipping and handling fef es billed to customers are included in net sales, while costs of shipping and
handling are included in cost of sales. Refef r to Note 24. Revenue for
f
fur
f
ther infor
f

Net income per share—Basic net income per share is computed by dividing net income attributabl
a e to ordinary
r
shareholders by the weighted average number of ordinary
r shares outstanding during the period. Diluted net income per share
reflf ects the weighted average dilutive impact of all potentially dilutive securities frf om the date of issuance and is computed
using the treasury
r stock and if-f converted methods. The if-f converted method is used to determine if the impact of conversion of
the 5.50% Mandatory
r Convertible Prefef rred Shares, Series A, $0.01 par value per share (the “MCPS”) into ordinary
r shares is
more dilutive than the MCPS dividends to net income per share. If so, the MCPS are assumed to have been converted at the
later of the beginning of the period or the time of issuance, and the resulting ordinary
r shares are included in the denominator
and the MCPS dividends are added back to the numerator. Unless otherwise noted, share and per share amounts included in
these notes are on a diluted basis. Refef r to Note 15. Shareholders’ Equity and Net Income Per Share for
f
additional infor
f
mation
including the calculation of basic and diluted net income per share.

Credit losses—Aptiv is exposed to credit losses primarily through the sale of vehicle components and services. Aptiv
assesses the creditworthiness of a counterpa
r

billing exposure and timing for
f

r

a ished credit rating. When a credit rating is not

r

r and political risk, and business strategy in its evaluation. Based on the outcome of this

f

r

r

Valuation of long-lived assets—The carryi

carryi

Leases—The Company accounts for
f
leases in accordance with FASB ASC Topic 842, Leases. The Company determines
whether an arrangement is a lease at inception. For leases where the Company is the lessee, a lease liabi
a lity and a right-of-f use
asset is recognized for
f
all leases, with the exception of short-term leases with terms of twelve months or less. The lease liabi
a lity
represents the lessee’s obligation to make lease payments arising frf om a lease, and is measured as the present value of the lease
payments. As the rate implicit in the lease is usually not known at lease commencement, the Company uses its incremental
borrowing rate to discount the lease obligation. The right-of-f use asset represents the lessee’s right to use a specifif ed asset for
f
the
lease term, and is measured at the lease liabi
a lity amount, adjusted for
f
lease prepayment, lease incentives received and the
Company’s initial direct costs.
The Company appl
a
ies the short-term lease exception, which results in a single lease cost being allocated over the lease
term, generally on a straight-line basis, for
f
leases with a term of twelve months or less. These leases are not presented in the
consolidated balance sheets. Additionally, the Company appl
a
ies the practical expedient to not separate lease components frf om
non-lease components and instead accounts for
f
both as a single lease component for
f
all asset classes. Refef r to Note 25. Leases
for
f
additional infor
f
mation.
Assets and liabilities held for
f

opriate authority, appr

a e net assets acquired in
business combinations. The Company tests goodwill for

the fif scal year. The Company tests for

of operating segments which constitut
t e businesses for
f
which discrete fif nancial infor
f

a e and is regularly

The impairment test involves fif rst qualitatively assessing goodwill for
f

the Company then perfor
f

carryi
r ng value, including goodwill. Fair value reflf ects the price a market participant would be willing to pay in a potential sale
of the reporting unit. If the estimated faf ir value exceeds carryi
r ng value, then we conclude that no goodwill impairment has
occurred. If the carryi
r ng value of the reporting unit exceeds its estimated faf ir value, the Company recognizes an impairment
loss in an amount equal to the excess, not to exceed the amount of goodwill allocated to the reporting unit. Refef r to Note 20.

Credit Agreement
Aptiv PLC and its wholly-owned subsidiary
r Aptiv Corpor
r
ation entered into a credit agreement (the “Credit Agreement”)
with JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A., as administrative agent (the “Administrative Agent”), under which it maintains senior
unsecured credit faf cilities currently consisting of a term loan (the “Tranche A Term Loan”) and a revolving credit faf cility of $2
billion (the “Revolving Credit Facility”). Subsequently, Aptiv Global Financing Limited (“AGFL”), a wholly-owned subsidiary
r
of Aptiv PLC, executed a joinder agreement to the Credit Agreement, which allows it to act as a borrower under the Credit

13. COMMITMENTS AND CONTINGENCIES
Ordinary Business Litigation
Aptiv is frf om time to time subject to various legal actions and claims incidental to its business, including those arising out
of alleged defef cts, alleged breaches of contracts, product warranties, intellectua
t
l property matters, and employment-related
matters. It is the opinion of Aptiv that the outcome of such matters will not have a material adverse impact on the consolidated
fif nancial position, results of operations, or cash flf ows of Aptiv. With respect to warranty matters, although Aptiv cannot ensure
that the fut
f ur
t
e costs of warranty claims by customers will not be material, Aptiv believes its establ
a ished reserves are adequate to
cover potential warranty settlements.
Matters Related to Global Supply Chain Disruptions
Due to various faf ctors that are beyond our control, there are currently global supply chain disrupt
r
ions, including a
worldwide semiconductor supply shortage. The semiconductor supply shortage, due in part to increased demand across multiple
industries, is impacting production in automotive and other industries. We anticipate these supply chain disrupt
r
ions will persist
in 2023. We, along with most automotive component manufaf ctur
t
ers that use semiconductors, have been unabl
a e to ful
f ly meet
the vehicle production demands of OEMs because of events which are outside our control, including but not limited to, the
COVID-19 pandemic, the global semiconductor shortage, fif res in our suppliers’ faf cilities, unprecedented weather events in the
southwestern United States, and other extraordinary
r events. Although we are working closely with suppliers and customers to
minimize any potential adverse impacts of these events, some of our customers have indicated that they expect us to bear at
least some responsibility for
f
their lost production and other costs. While no assurances can be made as to the ultimate outcome
of these customer expectations or any other fut
f ur
t
e claims, we do not currently believe a loss is probabl
a e, and accordingly, no
reserve has been made as of December 31, 2022. We will continue to actively monitor all direct and indirect potential impacts
of these supply chain disrupt
r
ions, and will seek to aggressively mitigate and minimize their impact on our business.
Brazil Matters
Aptiv conducts business operations in Brazil that are subject to the Brazilian fef deral labor
a
, social security, environmental,
health and safef ty, tax and customs laws, as well as a variety of state and local laws. While Aptiv believes it complies with such
laws, they are complex, subject to varyi
r ng interpr
r
etations, and the Company is oftf en engaged in litigation with government
agencies regarding the appl
a
ication of these laws to particular circumstances. As of December 31, 2022, the maja ority of claims
asserted against Aptiv in Brazil relate to such litigation. The remaining claims in Brazil relate to commercial and labor
a
litigation
with private parties. As of December 31, 2022, claims totaling appr
a
oximately $105 million (using December 31, 2022 for
f
eign
currency rates) have been asserted against Aptiv in Brazil. As of December 31, 2022, the Company maintains accrua
r
ls for
f
these
asserted claims of $5 million (using December 31, 2022 for
f
eign currency rates). The amounts accrue
r
d represent claims that are
deemed probabl
a e of loss and are reasonabl
a y estimabl
a e based on the Company’s analyses and assessment of the asserted claims
and prior experience with similar matters. While the Company believes its accrua
r
ls are adequate, the fif nal amounts required to
resolve these matters could difff ef r materially frf om the Company’s recorded estimates and Aptiv’s results of operations could be
99
materially afff ef cted. The Company estimates the reasonabl
a y possible loss in excess of the amounts accrue
r
d related to these
claims to be zero to $40 million.
Environmental Matters
Aptiv is subject to the requirements of U.S. fef deral, state, local and non-U.S. environmental, health and safef ty laws and
regulations. As of December 31, 2022 and 2021, the undiscounted reserve for

Net Income Per Share
Basic net income per share is computed by dividing net income attributabl
a e to ordinary
r shareholders by the weighted
average number of ordinary
r shares outstanding during the period. Diluted net income per share reflf ects the weighted average
dilutive impact of all potentially dilutive securities frf om the date of issuance and is computed using the treasury
r stock and if-f
converted methods. The if-f converted method is used to determine if the impact of the conversion of the MCPS into ordinary
r
shares is more dilutive than the MCPS dividends to net income per share. If so, the MCPS are assumed to have been converted
at the later of the beginning of the period or the time of issuance, and the resulting ordinary
r shares are included in the
denominator and the MCPS dividends are added back to the numerator. For the years ended December 31, 2022 and 2021, the
impact of the MCPS calculated under the if-f converted method was anti-dilutive, and as such 12.37 million and 12.37 million
ordinary
r shares underlying the MCPS, respectively, were excluded frf om the diluted net income per share calculation. For the
year ended December 31, 2020, the calculation of net income per share includes the dilutive impacts of the MCPS under the if-f
converted method. For all periods presented, the calculation of net income per share also contemplates the dilutive impacts, if
any, of the Company’s share-based compensation plans. Refef r to Note 21. Share-Based Compensation for
f
additional
infor
f
mation.

17. DERIVATIVES AND HEDGING ACTIVITIES
Cash Flow Hedges
Aptiv is exposed to market risk, such as flf uctua
t
tions in for
f
eign currency exchange rates, commodity prices and changes
in interest rates, which may result in cash flf ow risks. To manage the volatility relating to these exposures, Aptiv aggregates the
exposures on a consolidated basis to take advantage of natur
t
al offf sf ets. For exposures that are not offf sf et within its operations,
Aptiv enters into various derivative transactions pursuant to its risk management policies, which prohibit holding or issuing
derivative fif nancial instrum
r
ents for
f
speculative purpos
r
es, and designation of derivative instrum
r
ents is perfor
f
med on a
transaction basis to support hedge accounting. The changes in faf ir value of these hedging instrum
r
ents are offf sf et in part or in
108
whole by corresponding changes in the faf ir value or cash flf ows of the underlying exposures being hedged. Aptiv assesses the
initial and ongoing efff ef ctiveness of its hedging relationships in accordance with its documented policy.

Net Investment Hedges
The Company is also exposed to the risk that adverse changes in for
f
eign currency exchange rates could impact its net
investment in non-U.S. subsidiaries. To manage this risk, the Company designates certain qualifyi
f ng derivative and non-
derivative instrum
r
ents, including for
f
eign currency for
f
ward contracts and for
f
eign currency-denominated debt, as net investment
hedges of certain non-U.S. subsidiaries. The gains or losses on instrum
r
ents designated as net investment hedges are recognized
within OCI to offf sf et changes in the value of the net investment in these for
f
eign currency-denominated operations. Gains and
losses reported in accumulated OCI are reclassifif ed to earnings only when the related currency translation adjustments are
required to be reclassifif ed, usually upon sale or liquidation of the investment. Cash flf ows frf om derivatives designated as net
investment hedges are classifif ed as investing activities within the consolidated statements of cash flf ows.
The Company has entered into a series of for
f
ward contracts, each of which have been designated as net investment
hedges of the for
f
eign currency exposure of the Company’s investments in certain Chinese Yuan Renminbi (“RMB”)-
denominated subsidiaries. During the years ended December 31, 2022, 2021 and 2020, the Company received $7 million, and
made net payments of $17 million and $1 million, respectively, at settlement related to these series of for
f
ward contracts which
matur
t

f

amount of 700 million RMB (appr

f

18. FAIR VALUE OF FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
Fair value is defif ned as the exchange price that would be received to sell an asset or paid to transfef r a liabi
a lity (an exit
price) in the principal or most advantageous market for
f
the asset or liabi
a lity in an orderly transaction between market
participants on the measurement date. Fair value measurements are based on one or more of the fol
f lowing three valuation
techniques:
Mar
M
k
r e
k t—T
t
his appr
a
oach uses prices and other relevant infor
f
mation generated by market transactions involving identical or
comparabl
a e assets or liabi
a lities.
Inc
I
ome—This appr
a
oach uses valuation techniques to convert fut
f ur
t
e amounts to a single present value amount based on
current market expectations.
Cos
C
t—T
t
his appr
a
oach is based on the amount that would be required to replace the service capa
a
city of an asset
(replacement cost).
112
Aptiv uses the fol
f lowing faf ir value hierarchy prescribed by U.S. GAAP, which prioritizes the inputs used to measure faf ir
value as fol
f lows:
Level 1—Unadjusted quoted prices in active markets for
f
identical assets or liabi
a lities.
Level 2—Observabl
a e inputs other than Level 1 prices, such as quoted prices for
f
similar assets or liabi
a lities; quoted prices
in markets that are not active; or other inputs that are observabl

a e market data for

Level 3—Unobservabl
a e inputs that are supported by little or no market activity and that are signifif cant to the faf ir value of
the assets or liabi
a lities.
Typically, assets and liabi
a lities are considered to be faf ir valued on a recurring basis if faf ir value is measured regularly.
However, if the faf ir value measurement of an instrum
r
ent does not necessarily result in a change in the amount recorded on the
consolidated balance sheets, assets and liabi
a lities are considered to be faf ir valued on a nonrecurring basis. This generally occurs
when accounting guidance requires assets and liabi
a lities to be recorded at the lower of cost or faf ir value, or assessed for
f
impairment.

a

22. SEGMENT REPORTING
Aptiv operates its core business along the fol
f lowing operating segments, which are grouped on the basis of similar
product, market and operating faf ctors:
•
Signal and Power Solutions, which includes complete electrical architectur
t
e and component products.
•
Advanced Safef ty and User Experience, which includes vehicle technology and services in advanced safef ty, user
experience and connectivity and security solutions, as well as cloud-native softf ware platfor
f
ms, autonomous driving
technologies and DevOps tools.
•
Eliminations and Other, which includes i) the elimination of inter-segment transactions, and ii) certain other expenses
and income of a non-operating or strategic natur
t
e.
The accounting policies of the segments are the same as those described in Note 2. Signifif cant Accounting Policies,
except that the disaggregated fif nancial results for
f
the segments have been prepared using a management appr
a
oach, which is
consistent with the basis and manner in which management internally disaggregates fif nancial infor
f
mation for
f
which Aptiv’s
chief operating decision maker regularly reviews fif nancial results to assess perfor
f
mance of,f and make internal operating
decisions about
a
allocating resources to, the segments.
Generally, Aptiv evaluates segment perfor
f
mance based on stand-alone segment net income befor
f
e interest expense, other
income (expense), net, income tax (expense) benefif t, equity income (loss), net of tax, amortization, restruc
r
tur
t
ing, other
acquisition and portfol
f io project costs (which includes costs incurred to integrate acquired businesses and to plan and execute
product portfol
f io transfor
f
mation actions, including business and product acquisitions and divestitur
t
es), asset impairments and
other related charges and gains (losses) on business divestitur
t
es and other transactions (“Adjusted Operating Income”) and
accounts for
f
inter-segment sales and transfef rs as if the sales or transfef rs were to third parties, at current market prices.
123
Efff ef ctive on January
r 1, 2022, the Company now excludes amortization expense of intangible assets frf om the calculation
of Adjusted Operating Income, as reflf ected in the defif nition above
a
. The Company’s management believes that the updated
calculation of this fif nancial measure will be more useful
f
to both management and investors in their analysis of the Company’s
results of operations due to recent acquisitions. Amortization of intangible assets generally results frf om a write-up in the value
of assets in connection with an acquisition. The Company believes that exclusion of amortization expense will faf cilitate more
comparabl
a e operating results of the Company over time, between periods when the Company is more or less acquisitive and
allows for
f
improved comparison with both acquisitive and non-acquisitive peer companies. The historical presentation of
Adjusted Operating Income in the tabl
a es below has been revised to be consistent with this updated calculation.
Aptiv’s management utilizes Adjusted Operating Income as the key perfor
f
mance measure of segment income or loss to
evaluate segment perfor
f
mance, and for
f
planning and for
f
ecasting purpos
r
es to allocate resources to the segments, as management
believes this measure is most reflf ective of the operational profif tabi
a lity or loss of Aptiv’s operating segments. Segment Adjusted
Operating Income should not be considered a substitut
t e for
f
results prepared in accordance with U.S. GAAP and should not be
considered an alternative to net income attributabl
a e to Aptiv, which is the most directly comparabl
a e fif nancial measure to
Adjusted Operating Income that is prepared in accordance with U.S. GAAP. Segment Adjusted Operating Income, as
determined and measured by Aptiv, should also not be compared to similarly titled measures reported by other companies.

24. REVENUE
Refef r to Note 2. Signifif cant Accounting Policies for
f
a complete description of the Company’s revenue recognition
accounting policy.
Nature of Goods and Services
The principal activity frf om which the Company generates its revenue is the manufaf ctur
t
ing of production parts for
f
OEM
customers. Aptiv recognizes revenue for
f
production parts at a point in time, rather than over time, as the perfor
f
mance obligation
is satisfif ed when customers obtain control of the product upon title transfef r and not as the product is manufaf ctur
t
ed or
developed.
Although production parts are highly customized with no alternative use, Aptiv does not have an enfor
f
ceabl
a e right to
payment as customers have the right to cancel a product program without a notifif cation period. The amount of revenue
recognized is based on the purchase order price and adjusted for
f
revenue allocated to variabl
a e consideration (i.e. estimated
rebates and price discounts), as appl
a
icabl
a e. Customers typically pay for
f
production parts based on customary
r business practices
with payment terms averaging 60 days.
The Company also generates revenue frf om the sale of softf ware licenses, post delivery
r support and maintenance and
profef ssional softf ware services, primarily frf om Wind River, which the Company acquired in December 2022. Refef r to Note 20.
Acquisitions and Divestitur
t
es for
f
fur
f
ther infor
f
mation on this acquisition. The Company generally recognizes revenue for
f
softf ware licenses and profef ssional softf ware services at a point in time upon delivery
r or when the services are provided.
Revenue frf om post delivery
r support and maintenance for
f
softf ware contracts are recognized over time on a ratabl
a e basis over the
contract term. Under certain of these arrangements, timing may difff ef r between revenue recognition and billing.

Remaining Perfor
f
mance Obligations
For production parts, customer contracts generally are represented by a combination of a current purchase order and a
current production schedule issued by the customer. There are no contracts for
f
production parts outstanding beyond one year.
129
Aptiv does not enter into fif xed long-term supply agreements. As permitted, Aptiv does not disclose infor
f
mation about
a
remaining perfor
f
mance obligations that have original expected durations of one year or less for
f
production parts.
Customer contracts for
f
sales of softf ware and related services are generally represented by a sales contract or purchase
order with contract durations typically ranging frf om one to three years. Remaining perfor
f
mance obligations include contract
liabi
a lities and unbilled amounts that will be recognized as revenue in fut
f ur
t
e periods. Transaction price allocated to the
remaining perfor
f
mance obligation is based on the standalone selling price. The value of the transaction price allocated to
remaining perfor
f
mance obligations under softf ware and related service contracts as of December 31, 2022 was appr
a
oximately
$135 million. The Company expects to recognize appr
a
oximately 67% of remaining perfor
f
mance obligations as revenue in the
next twelve months, and the remainder thereaftf er.
Costs to Obtain a Contract
From time to time, Aptiv makes payments to customers in conjunction with ongoing business. These payments to
customers are generally recognized as a reduction to revenue at the time of the commitment to make these payments. However,
certain other payments to customers, or upfrf ont fef es, meet the criteria to be considered a cost to obtain a contract as they are
directly attributabl
a e to a contract, are incremental and management expects the fef es to be recoverabl
a e. As of December 31, 2022
and 2021, Aptiv has recorded $78 million (of which $17 million was classifif ed within other current assets and $61 million was
classifif ed within other long-term assets) and $92 million (of which $34 million was classifif ed within other current assets and
$58 million was classifif ed within other long-term assets), respectively, related to these capi
a talized upfrf ont fef es.
Capi
a talized upfrf ont fef es are amortized to revenue based on the transfef r of goods and services to the customer for
f
which
the upfrf ont fef es relate, which typically range frf om three to fif ve years. There have been no impairment losses in relation to the
costs capi
a talized. The amount of amortization to net sales was $28 million, $31 million and $18 million for
f
the years ended
December 31, 2022, 2021 and 2020, respectively.
25. LEASES
Lease Portfol
f io
The Company has operating and fif nance leases for
f
real estate, offf if ce equipment, automobiles, for
f
kliftf s and certain other
equipment. The Company's leases have remaining lease terms of one year to 30 years, some of which include options to extend
the leases for
f
up to eight years, and some of which include options to terminate the leases within one year. Certain of our lease
agreements include rental payments which are adjusted periodically for
f
inflf ation. Our lease agreements do not contain any
material residual value guarantees or material restrictive covenants. When availabl
a e, we use the rate implicit in the lease to
discount lease payments to present value; however, most of our leases do not provide a readily determinabl
a e implicit rate.
Therefor
f
e, we must estimate our incremental borrowing rate to discount the lease payments based on infor
f
mation availabl
a e at
lease commencement. The incremental borrowing rate is not a quoted rate and is primarily derived by appl
a
ying a spread over
U.S. Treasury
r rates with a similar duration to the Company’s lease payments. The spread utilized is based on the Company’s
credit rating and the impact of ful
f l collateralization.

t

of appr

ITEM 9A. CONTROLS AND PROCEDURES
Evaluation of Disclosure Controls and Procedures
Management of the Company, under the supervision and with the participation of the Chief Executive Offf if cer and the
Chief Financial Offf if cer, carried out an evaluation of the efff ef ctiveness of the design and operation of the Company’s disclosure
controls and procedures as of December 31, 2022. As defif ned in Rul
R e 13a-15(e) under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 (the
“Exchange Act”), disclosure controls and procedures are controls and procedures designed to provide reasonabl
a e assurance that
infor
f
mation required to be disclosed in reports fif led or submitted under the Exchange Act is recorded, processed, summarized
and reported on a timely basis, and that such infor
f
mation is accumulated and communicated to management, including the
Company’s Chief Executive Offf if cer and Chief Financial Offf if cer, as appr
a
opriate, to allow timely decisions regarding required
disclosure. The Company’s disclosure controls and procedures include components of the Company’s internal control over
fif nancial reporting. Based upon this evaluation, the Chief Executive Offf if cer and Chief Financial Offf if cer have concluded that the
Company’s disclosure controls and procedures were efff ef ctive as of December 31, 2022.
Management’s Report on Internal Control Over Financial Reporting
Management is responsible for
f
establ
a ishing and maintaining adequate internal control over fif nancial reporting, as such
term is defif ned in Exchange Act Rul
R es 13a-15(f)f and 15d-15(f)f , for
f
the Company. Under the supervision of the Chief Executive
Offf if cer and Chief Financial Offf if cer, management conducted an evaluation of the efff ef ctiveness of the Company’s internal
control over fif nancial reporting as of December 31, 2022 based on the frf amework set for
f
th by the Committee of Sponsoring
Organizations of the Treadway Commission (COSO) in “Internal Control-Integrated Framework (2013).” Based on that
evaluation, management has concluded that the Company’s internal control over fif nancial reporting was efff ef ctive as of
December 31, 2022. Due to the timing of each respective acquisition during the four
f
th quarter of 2022, the Company has
excluded the acquired operations of Wind River Systems, Inc. (“Wind River”) and Intercabl
a e Automotive S.r.l. (“Intercabl
a e
Automotive”) frf om its assessment of the efff ef ctiveness of the Company’s internal controls over fif nancial reporting. Wind River
and Intercabl
a e Automotive represented appr
a
oximately 23% of the Company’s assets as of December 31, 2022 and less than 1%
of net sales and net income for
f
the year ended December 31, 2022.
Ernst & Young LLP has issued an attestation report which is included herein as the Report of Independent Registered
Public Accounting Firm under the section headed Financial Statements and Supplementary
r Data for
f
the year ended
December 31, 2022.
Changes in Internal Control Over Financial Reporting
There were no material changes in the Company’s internal control over fif nancial reporting, identifif ed in connection with
management’s evaluation of internal control over fif nancial reporting, that occurred during the quarter and year ended
December 31, 2022 that have materially afff ef cted, or are reasonabl
a y likely to materially afff ef ct, the Company’s internal control
over fif nancial reporting. The Company is integrating Wind River and Intercabl
a e Automotive into the Company’s operations,
compliance programs and internal control processes. Specifif cally, as permitted by SEC rul
r es and regulations, the Company has
excluded Wind River and Intercabl
a e Automotive frf om management’s evaluation of internal controls over fif nancial reporting as
of December 31, 2022.
